How Zara Larsson’s MUA Makes Pink Eyeshadow Chic, Not Costumey

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Think pink eyeshadow isn't wearable? Zara Larsson's makeup artist Ernesto Casillas taught us exactly how you can pull off the bold hue.

Zara Larsson has proven that her influence as a pop girlie is never ending—and that all comes down to her bubbly energy and playful fashion choices.

The Swedish singer's Y2K-inspired stage looks consisting of bold colors and light-catching shimmer aren't complete without her equally fun makeup looks.

While she's not one to shy away from color, at the Billboard Women in Music event, Zara's makeup artist Ernesto Casillas wanted to create a "softer, feminine, but...still somewhat colorful," eye look that could transition from the red carpet to the stage.

Casillas exclusively shared with E! how he created Zara's "Malibu Barbie" eye look.

If pink eyeshadow feels too punchy for regular wear, Casillas told E! exactly how he makes it look chic rather than costumey.

"I had fun by using pinks, just more wearable pinks," he said. Think "frosted champagne pinks on the lid, maybe a little blush on the crease."

Another key is to craft your preferred shape using more neutral hues. Casillas suggests "A pinky brown eyeshadow through the crease, just to softly contour it. But I wanted it to be a little more pink. So I dipped into a little blush, and softly went over the crease, just to warm it up even more."

This affordable three-pan palette has everything you need to recreate Zara's look, including a warm brown crease color, soft pink lid shade, and shimmery topper.

For more variety, you can opt for this 12-pan palette packed with highly-pigmented shimmers and metallics in addition to blendable matte transition shades.

To add a little extra punch, Ernesto suggests lightly dipping a shadow brush into a bold pink blush to deepen the crease. This monochromatic trio includes a satin and velvet powder plus a luminous balm. Apply it to your cheeks for a cohesive look.

"I knew she would be on stage, so I knew to play up, glossy and frosty textures, because the light will pick up all that beautiful shine on the eyelids, on the cheeks, and on the lips," shared Casillas.

To amplify Zara's subtle pink eyeshadow, he made sure to add shimmer at the center of the lids, along the cheekbones, paired with a glossy lip.

Intensify a soft pink eye look by adding a shimmery topper to the center and inner corner of your eyes. This one from Half Magic offers an intense color payoff in just a few swipes.

To bring the whole look together, Casillas added a few lash clusters from Lashify to define Zara's cat-like eye shape.

"I placed [the Stardust style] on the outside of her eye, because she likes a very feline eye shape. So I did that to accentuate her shape," he said. "Then I did the spike, [using] the Cherry Stax across the entire eye to give her a dolly look."

Lashify's individual clusters allow you to customize your perfect fake lash. Available in several colors and lengths, concentrate them on the outer ends of the eye to produce an elongated effect.

Pair Lashify's clusters with their powerful long-hold adhesive with a barely there feel.

Fenty Beauty's iconic gloss bomb in the bestselling shade FU$$Y is the perfect finishing touch to your Zara-inspired look. It delivers a “your lips but better” wash with a hint of light-catching shimmer.

EXCLUSIVE: Ryan Serhant Warns Homebuyers Against Waiting for 2027: ‘You Don’t Want to Wait Until It’s Too Late’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ryan Serhant is warning would-be homebuyers against assuming that lower mortgage rates will deliver an easier path to homeownership in 2027—and says the housing market has a problem that cheaper borrowing alone cannot solve.

Speaking exclusively to Realtor.com®, Serhant shared his outlook for the year ahead, identified markets he believes could surprise buyers, and weighed in on the debate over private listings.

He also offered a glimpse inside his own townhouse and revealed a buyer request that has him searching for an enormous stretch of land with its own airport.

At the center of his housing outlook is a concern about people at opposite ends of the property ladder being unable to move.

“We have entered an environment where people can't afford to buy their first home, and their parents can't afford to leave their last one, and it's a problem we need to solve," he said.

“Lower mortgage rates will help, but rates alone are not going to fix the cost of housing or the cost of moving. We need to create more supply and fewer obstacles," Serhant added.

Serhant argued that the system has become an obstacle to moving, with consequences that have developed over many years.

“I think that what we've created is a environment, that is punishing mobility. Instead of rewarding mobility. And this was set up a long, long time ago. We didn't get here overnight.”

“Like, the housing market and the system we operate in rewards stability, because what's… it makes everyone feel comfortable. But then when that stability does not move, because stability is stability, mobility is mobility. Then everyone gets angry, and that's the environment that we're currently in.”

For his outlook, he is watching whether households have both the confidence and the resources to act.

“I think I'm watching employment, and I'm watching household confidence right now. I think pent-up demand only becomes a transaction when people have the confidence and the financial ability to act, and we'll see if that happens anytime soon.”

For people who can afford to buy but are considering waiting for a better deal in 2027, Serhant pointed to opportunities he believes already exist.

“I think in a lot of markets, we've entered a buyer's market now. You have the ability to negotiate in markets that you haven't been able to negotiate in in the past couple years.”

“I think there's a great opportunity to use the rate pressure that we have to negotiate closing costs, to negotiate rate buy-downs, to negotiate lower purchase prices, to get a house that really, really, really works for you. Remember, it is such an old and annoying saying, but you do marry the house, and you do date through the rates.”

His argument rests on the potential trade-off between borrowing costs and competition, although his comments about future rates and inventory are predictions.

“The market does become very stagnant. Interest rates should come down, and remember, you can refinance. And you should use the opportunity right now, if you can afford it, to purchase in a high-interest rate environment, because there's less competition.”

“Everyone wants low rates, they never want what comes with it. You know what'll happen if rates come down? There will be no houses for sale.”

“You won't even have the opportunity to complain about a high monthly payment. You won't even be able to talk about—the lack of affordability. You won't be able to complain, because there will be nothing for sale, because the demand is so great. There is not nearly enough houses for the demand we have in this country. Every 40-year-old who still lives at home will then move.”

Serhant also argued that the circumstances accompanying lower rates can make buyers reluctant to purchase.

“And what also brings down low interest rates is job insecurity. You know, is unemployment, is war. Are things that happen in the global environment that will make people, not confident to go and make a massive purchase. And so, you might miss that opportunity, too.”

He used the early months of the pandemic to illustrate his point about confidence and timing.

“In the spring of 2020, that was a great time to buy. But no, we had COVID, 'You can't buy. There's a death ticker on the bottom of every TV screen. You might not live.' You didn't take advantage of this environment, and then when the market came back, it was too late.”

“And so, I would use this moment to move quickly, because when things do even out, and they always do, markets go up and markets go down. Always. And forever. You don't want to wait until it's too late.”

Serhant highlighted returning city residents and investment in jobs as themes behind his market picks.

“I think people are returning to cities in a way that we haven't seen before. It's one of the reasons we just were launching, officially Chicago. This coming Wednesday. I'm incredibly excited about that.”

“The demand and the volume we've seen from all of our other Serhant markets wanting to move back to Chicago, who moved out over the past couple years. Taking advantage of good value, like we just talked about, is really exciting.”

“And I think places where you're seeing incredible job growth. Areas like Ohio, where there's major, major tech investment, not just in data centers. In office. And in manufacturing.”

“We have a lot of investment that's going into Western Pennsylvania, and, you're also seeing a lot of movement into Alabama.”

“You know, I think there are markets that will really, really surprise you because of great quality of life for the best cost structure the country has to offer.”

Serhant also addressed private listings, beginning with his explanation of how they differ from properties marketed publicly.

“A private listing is the opposite of a public to the consumer listing," he said.

“It has been the standard over the past… 20-some-odd years—that if you want to market your home, so that there is a fair and free market and competitive marketplace, that if you opt to work with a real estate agent. That you have to bring your home to the public marketplace.”

“Obviously, every home that's not on the market is technically a private listing. The Empire State Building is a private listing, for what it's worth. The whole world is a private listing.”

He then offered his criticism of listing restrictions and penalties. The following is his characterization of the debate, rather than a description of a single policy applying to every platform or multiple listing service.

“But what has changed now is the MLSs, and the listing aggregators have installed fees and penalties.”

“So, if you list with them, as you're supposed to, you can have the prize of being public, but if you don't list with them, because you want to work with an agent, but you don't want your home to be blasted all over the world, then you can never list with them ever, and we will also punish you, potentially with fines.”

“The current debate is over, should that be allowed? Who actually owns the data, and who actually owns the property? And access to it. Is it the brokerage? Is it the websites that showcase listings? Is it the multiple listing services? Is it the owner of the property?”

For Serhant, the discussion should focus on what sellers receive from the process.

“And that's what the debate is over right now, is what they say, consumer choice. I think what is broken is that it's not about consumer choice. I think everyone's focused on the wrong thing. I think people should be focused on consumer value.”

“Where's the upside? Where's the consumer upside? You know, the battle right now is over commission fees, and who gets a piece. And I think that's gross, and I'm in this business.”

“My position is pretty simple. I think your home deserves the whole market if you'd like it to. And if a seller genuinely wants privacy, we need to respect that. But then you have to explain the trade-off. Privacy and maximum exposure are different objectives.”

On concerns that private listings could reduce a seller's pool of buyers or disadvantage people without the right connections, Serhant acknowledged the risks.

“I think there are real concerns. But I think everybody has to be fully aware of what those concerns are. And again, I think it should be up to the consumer, and unfortunately, these rules exist because in a world where there is no rules, and there is no punishment. Not everyone is a good actor.”

He drew on his own experience working as an agent during the financial crisis.

“Like, all the people that are leading this fight, none of them were real estate agents like I was in 2008 and 2009. None of them were trying to get business on Craigslist. None of them were trying to figure out what was for sale and what wasn't for sale.”

“None of them were trying to figure out if the listing agent in front of them was lying to their client or not. None of them were trying to figure out, with the fiduciary responsibility of a buyer in front of you, how to give them the most accurate and up-to-date information.”

“None of them were, like me, on the streets of New York City, or trying to do deals in Florida and California, trying to figure out what is actually for sale, and if a buyer isn't paying an excise commission by working with that agent, they actually won't even get that property? Is that really? What we want to go back to?”

“I feel like a homeowner's value and a homeowner's biggest asset shouldn't become a real estate brokerage's, or an MLS's, or a listing aggregator's recruiting tool.”

Away from the business of buying and selling, Serhant shared the first thing he does when he walks into his townhouse.

“I yell out my daughter's name. I wanted to see where she is in my house. We have a townhouse, so I'm trying to figure out which floor she's on, so I can, you know, prepare.”

His favorite spaces include a gym on the lower level, although his affection extends to the whole property.

“I have a pretty awesome gym. In the lower level that I built out, that's really, really cool, and I think that's pretty awesome, but I mean, I built it with my bare hand. It was a TV show that we did for Bravo about our renovation, and I love that house. Whole thing is great.”

He also described the practical details that help make his kitchen work, starting with a concealed ladder for reaching high cabinets.

“We have incredibly high ceilings. So we have a hidden ladder in our kitchen that looks like the baseboard under the kitchen. Island, and it pops out with the touch of your toe.”

“And, the ladder shows up, and so for anyone, you know, you know, who's vertically challenged to be able to reach upper cabinetry without having a ladder in front of your face at all times, so that you can still enjoy all that light and sunshine and high ceilings, I think is key.”

“Probably the greatest things that we have also in that kitchen are touchless, so, like, the sink, you know, you're cooking, and your hands are messy, and you're always hitting the thing. We have the kitchen sink, where all you have to do is tap it, and it turns on.”

“And so you just hit it with the back of your hand, and it turns on. So you're not, like, also getting the sink and the faucet dirty.”

“And then the same thing for the kitchen refrigerator and freezer. All you do is you slowly kind of, like, tap it with your elbow, or with the side, so you're not, like, yanking on anything, and it automatically just reveals itself to you. I think those little touches are overlooked.”

Among the unusual requests Serhant receives, one current search involves a vast amount of land—and a private airport.

“They're all wild. I'm dealing with somebody right now that wants 100,000 acres of land, which is impossible, for the most part."

For all the features a buyer might want, Serhant has one clear priority when choosing a home.

“You can change everything about the house you want over the years, you can grow into it, you can grow out of it, you can update it, you can paint it, you can flip it upside down. Unless you are Ken Griffin, and you can pick up a house, and you can move it, for the most part. Change the location.”

“And so, you always buy location first. Whatever that is, wherever that place is you want to be for the next 5-10 years, and most times you're paying more. But no one ever paid too much. And regretted it years later. Very, very rarely does that happen.”

“Remember, if you buy a trophy, you get to sell a trophy. If you buy a discount, because you're cheap, you will have to sell a discount. And you will feel cheap.”

Apple explains how to turn off the new MacBook Pro’s glowing logo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

While the illuminated Apple logo on the new MacBook Pro drew one of the biggest cheers during last week’s announcement, Apple knows that not everyone will want to keep it on. Here’s the company’s official guide to turning it off.

The new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display marks the return of the illuminated Apple logo on its lid after a decade.

As Chance Miller explained in his review of the new MacBook Pro, “unlike older MacBooks, which used light from the display’s backlight to illuminate the logo, the new OLED MacBook Pro has no backlight to draw from, requiring a different approach.”

He added that, as with the old Macs, the logo’s brightness is tied to the screen brightness, though it also accounts for overall ambient brightness. He also noted that “when the MacBook Pro is closed, the logo looks like polished metal, just like it has since the 2016 redesign.”

The review echoed some of the findings developer Gui Rambo shared on X a few days ago:

The glowing Apple logo on the new MacBook Pro is controllable by software. It has 25kHz PWM and per-device calibration. CoreBrightness controls the relationship between screen and logo brightness. The brightness of the logo is adjusted differently depending on the enclosure color pic.twitter.com/baqBEkzJUo

Now, Apple has published an official support document that also explains how the illuminated logo works and how to turn it off completely.

The Apple logo behind the built-in display is illuminated whenever the Mac is awake. It automatically dims or brightens based on the brightness level of the built-in screen and the lighting conditions detected by the computer’s ambient light sensor. For all screen-brightness levels, the Apple logo will be dimmer in a dim room and brighter in a bright room.

For users who’d rather keep the logo turned off at all times, Apple explains that they can run the following command in Terminal, provided they have the administrator password:

sudo defaults write com.apple.CoreBrightness LEBacklightMode -int 0

To revert the command and have the logo be illuminated again, users will need to run the following command:

sudo defaults delete com.apple.CoreBrightness LEBacklightMode

Apple notes that reinstalling macOS will also turn the logo illumination back on.

To read Apple’s full support article, follow this link. And make sure to also catch 9to5Mac’s review of the new MacBook Pro.

For Natural Volume, Wash Your Hair The French Girl Way

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

We all know that French girls have that special je ne sais quoi (so much so that it’s inspired countless essays on this very site). When it comes to their hair, the style is nonchalant—and yet, somehow also skillfully cultivated. It’s hair that’s air-dries, falls just right, and retains that slightly tousled movement that gives no hint of effort or styling. So how exactly do Jeanne Damas, Brigitte Bardot, Jane Birkin, and more do it?

Hairstylist and French girl herself Daphné Narcy has an idea: It all starts how you wash your hair. “You may be damaging your hair without even realizing it while in the shower,” Narcy says. “When you’re hair is wet, it’s at its most fragile. But habits like washing hair too quickly, scrubbing too hard, or using water that’s too hot may be causing breakage and frizz.”

She offers up eight simple shower-time adjustments to turn your hair into a French girl dream.

The first—and by far the most common—mistake: People wash their hair instead of their scalp. “We rub the lengths against each other, which damages them, and we neglect the roots, where sebum, pollution, and residue accumulate,” says Narcy. Her philosophy can be summed up in one sentence: A healthy scalp is the foundation of healthy hair. It’s also what determines how light the roots feel once the hair is dry.

The heat from a scalding shower lifts the hair cuticle, which can be bad for shine. “The hair loses moisture, becomes more porous and duller, and the color fades faster,” Narcy says. Hot water hurts the scalp, too, making it drier and more irritated. “Water that’s too hot also softens the hair follicles, which can lead to hair loss.” Your new rule of thumb here is disarmingly simple: “If the water feels pleasant on your wrist, without feeling hot, it’s the right temperature for your hair.”

Use your fingertips in small circular motions, without ever scrubbing. “It’s meant to be an invigorating massage, not a scrub,” insists Narcy. It’s also a moment of pleasantness. When you enjoy it, washing your hair becomes a ritual rather than a chore.

If you’ve ever read the back of a shampoo bottle, you know the instructions: Lather, rinse, repeat. And Narcy says that isn’t just to get you to use more product; but instead the double-shampoo is key to keeping hair clean—though only use a walnut-sized amount each time.

“When you use too much shampoo, residue can remain on the scalp, weighing it down, potentially irritating it, and making your hair look dirty faster The result: you end up washing your hair more often, and you get caught in a vicious cycle.”

The lengths should only be lightly touched when it comes to shampooing. “Press your hands together and slide them down, using very light and gentle friction, all the way to the ends. While rinsing, the lather that runs down finishes cleansing the lengths.” Less friction means fewer tangles, less breakage, and mid-lengths that not only stay hydrated but also dry smooth.

After EA, Silver Lake and PIF Reportedly Want Unity

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Contributor at DualShockers, former Esports Insider DACH content lead, former Escapist Magazine Germany content lead, software engineering background.

Unity Software has held talks to be acquired by a consortium that includes private equity firm Silver Lake and Saudi Arabia’s Public Investment Fund, according to Reuters. The group discussed a valuation of around $25 billion, whilst Unity’s market cap sits at roughly $21.5 billion after its latest earnings report. Shares rose about 4% in extended trading once the story broke.

Nothing is certain. The approach came in recent weeks, and Unity has reportedly been working with an investment bank to weigh its options ever since. Reuters notes that the plan and terms could still change, and Unity declined to comment.

Silver Lake and PIF have done this before. Both were part of the consortium behind the $55 billion EA take-private that closed in August, which ranks among the largest leveraged buyouts ever. A Unity deal would be their second enormous gaming bet in a matter of months.

Silver Lake isn’t walking in cold, either. It invested up to $400 million in Unity back in 2017 and still owns around 8% of the company. Its Egon Durban sits on Unity’s board, a dual role that would normally see him recuse himself from any board discussion of a sale. That’s the kind of detail that looks tidy on paper and gets picked at later.

Most people know Unity as the engine behind thousands of mobile, console and PC games, but its reach goes further than that. It’s used in automotive design, filmmaking, architecture and industrial simulation too. The company started out in Denmark in 2004, went public in September 2020, and has since wandered into advertising technology.

That advertising side is currently pulling its weight. Unity’s third quarter beat estimates, with revenue up 32% to $622.1 million and adjusted earnings of 44 cents per share. Strong growth in its AI-powered Vector platform helped it post its first quarterly profit on a GAAP basis since the IPO, alongside a solid showing from its game-development business. The takeover report landed straight afterwards. A company that has just turned its first profit is a much easier sell to a buyer, and I doubt that’s a coincidence.

There’s a practical angle too. According to Reuters, Unity’s tools are already used by Scopely, the developer behind Monopoly Go!, and Niantic, the creator of Pokémon Go, both of which are owned by a group that includes PIF, Silver Lake and Affinity Partners. The buyers would be acquiring something their own studios already rely on.

PIF’s route into gaming mostly runs through Savvy Games Group, which has had a mandate to invest $38 billion in the sector since 2022. Savvy bought Scopely for $4.9 billion in 2023, and Scopely then bought Niantic’s games business, Pokémon Go included, for $3.5 billion. ESL and FACEIT sit under the same umbrella. In January, PIF moved around $12 billion in listed gaming shares across to Savvy, including its stakes in Nintendo and Bandai Namco, followed by its roughly $3 billion Take-Two stake a month later. Add the EA deal on top and the list of major publishers with no Saudi money attached gets shorter every year.

The group discussed a valuation of around $25 billion, whilst Unity’s market cap sits at roughly $21.5 billion after its latest earnings report.

Savvy’s chief, Brian Ward, said at GDC that it doesn’t plan to use those stakes to push publishers into big changes. Taking him at his word, a minority shareholder is one thing. Owning the engine that other studios build on, including studios that compete with your own, is a different kind of position entirely.

Unity has been here before with developer trust. In September 2023 it announced a Runtime Fee that charged studios per install, and the backlash was fierce enough that CEO John Riccitiello stepped down within weeks. The fee was scrapped completely in September 2024, and current CEO Matthew Bromberg later admitted Unity had been “practically at war with our customers”. A leveraged buyout means debt, and debt has to be paid from somewhere. Developers are going to want to know where.

Right now, none of that is more than speculation. There are talks, an investment bank, and a number somewhere north of where the stock was trading. Studios will be watching to see which of those three moves first.

You Need A New Car? Go Electric! It’s Superior Technology! Part 1

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Your car is getting “long in tooth,” or maybe your daughter is going away to college and you are giving her your car. Whatever the reason, you are looking for another car. It could be new, it could be used: don’t even look at gas cars, electric cars are superior technology!

The wheels on every railroad locomotive in the world are driven by electric motors. All the busiest rail routes in Europe and China are pure electric, including high-speed rail. 98% of new cars in Norway are electric, over 80% of new cars in Denmark are electric. In China, the country with the largest sales of new cars in the world, the percentage of new electric cars in September 2026 was 67%. 82% of the operating bus fleet in China is electric. Even ships and airplanes are going electric. The data is unequivocal: electric propulsion is superior to internal combustion propulsion. Only the huge political contributions to Trump by the fossil fuel industry and his resulting anti-EV policies have slowed down adoption in this country.

In places we’ve been like California; Portland, Oregon; and northern Utah, you are seeing a lot of Teslas, so you know that electric cars are working for a lot of people these days. It’s time for you to do some research on electric cars.

You do pay a premium for new electric cars, because the batteries are still quite expensive. If price is the driving factor for you, buy a used electric car. Hardly any used EVs are high mileage, and EV batteries still have plenty of life even after they go off warranty at 8 years and 120,000 miles. There are thousands of lightly used EVs sitting at used car lots at reasonable prices all over the country these days. Some used car dealers will give you an estimate of the battery life. If not, get a friend who is an experienced EV driver to help you evaluate the battery life of the EV you are considering.

If you just do local driving or have two cars and have a garage or off-street parking, an EV is a no brainer. EVs are hands-down more convenient than gas cars for local driving. Your EV will have plenty of range for local driving, it will get a complete charge overnight in your home garage refueling station and it will never need an oil change.

EVs are as quiet as mice, as smooth as kittens, and as quick as jackrabbits. They have simple electric motors, not complex internal combustion engines. They have simple one-speed transmissions, not complex 7-speed automatic transmissions. They don’t have exhaust systems with mufflers that will rust and need replacement. They don’t have emission control systems with limited life. They don’t have complex cooling systems with big radiators. You seldom use your brakes with an EV, so your brakes may last the life of the car. They don’t have a big lead-acid battery for starting, with a maximum life of 5 years. New EVs have only a small lithium-ion battery, which lasts 10+ years. Bottom line: Routine maintenance for EVs is almost nil. I think the only real maintenance item on a Tesla over the first 3 years is the cabin air filter. You can expect an EV to go 500,000 miles without major repair or replacement in most cases.

Perhaps you can stomach buying a car from Tesla whose CEO has become one of the most despicable persons on the planet lately. (Example: Musk recently suspended access to his Starlink communications system to the Ukrainians who are valiantly defending their country from the Russian aggressors.) However, Tesla has sold almost 10 million automobiles worldwide that don’t emit CO2 and particulate pollution while operating. Tesla also has by far the most ubiquitous charging system, which allows cross-country driving on major US roads with little planning. Consider buying a Tesla.

So, what about cross-country trips? Everyone knows that long-distance trips in an electric car are inconvenient or even impossible, because there aren’t enough charging stations and it takes an hour if you can find one. Wrong: Every 100 miles along all the major Interstate highways, Tesla has built Superchargers with 8 to 32 and a few with over 100 charging stalls. By the time you have gone to the bathroom, walked the dog, and chatted with a fellow EV owner for a few minutes, you probably have more than enough charge to continue your journey. Usually, charging takes about 20 minutes. If you need a longer charge, you may need to watch a bit of Octopus Teacher or Back to the Future II on the big screen in the car for another 10 minutes.

News flash! Since February 28, 2023, most Tesla Superchargers (now North American Charging Standard/NACS chargers) are usable by all EV brands. Unfortunately, older Tesla Superchargers can’t be used by other brands, so I would still advise buying a Tesla if you want carefree cross-country driving. CCS chargers which are native to most other brands have sprung up by the thousands in the US, which together with the newer Tesla Superchargers make it possible to travel cross-country with non-Teslas on many routes with some planning. Also, Teslas can use non-Tesla chargers. Non-Tesla chargers often come with Tesla cables now, so Tesla owners don’t even need an adapter in most cases. However, even Teslas require careful planning if you are driving far from major highways.

My wife and I have done cross-country trips from Utah to Wisconsin for 7 years, on to North Carolina (once), and back to Utah. We have also traveled from Utah to Southern California at the same 500 miles per day rate that we used to do with our gas cars … and with no worries, mate.

I’m going to try to pass on some things I’ve learned from driving five different electric vehicles over 12 years and some 200,000 miles. See part two, coming soon.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order). Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get two months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months free Full Self Driving. It will be $99/month after that. For a Cybertruck, you will get $1000 off your purchase price. At this moment, Tesla is offering a year of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Model 3 Premium or Performance.

Also: Research Meteorologist (Emeritus) at NASA GSFC, Adjunct Professor at Viterbo University On-Line Studies, PSIA L2 Certified Alpine Ski Instructor at Brighton Utah Ski School.

Nile Rodgers Defends AI Use in Music Video: ‘We Created Everything … The AI Did What We Told It to Do’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Nile Rodgers recently shared a snippet of his upcoming video for a new version of “The Christmas Song,” his 2020 charity collaboration with Rebecca Ferguson, proudly revealing it used AI, which fans took issue with. On Thursday, he defended his use of the technology.

Earlier this week, he shared an “AI capture” of the project. “#working on a #great #art project that’s next #level. This is the start of the process. This AI capture was very thorough and I’m so excited about the finished project,” he wrote. “When I saw the Eagles at The Sphere in Vegas I envisioned stories with incredible environments & music. It’s future time!”

Shortly afterward, however, people began commenting on their disappointment with him using AI. One person wrote in the IG comments, “There’s better way to preserve your legacy.” Rodgers responded, “Wait to you see it before you make judgement. You’re imagining the worst instead of imagining the best.” The person responded back: “I don’t have to imagine my friend, look what AI has done to art in general yet alone music. Promoting it in such a big way is not the way to go.”

On Wednesday, Rodgers shared photo stills from the project, and the backlash continued. “We need happiness and healing in our world,” he captioned the carousel. “When I had my 1st bought of #cancer it was a child who coached me and gave me #courage to fight. I know a lot of children who are bedridden and ill. Their main form of entertainment is the iPad or other such devices. I produced ‘The Christmas Song’ to give them a little #joy during the holiday season.”He added: “We gave the #video a fun twist on the story line…and in the process expanded a new creative family for me. Over the next few weeks while we finish this video I’ll show bits and pieces while we rush to get it ready by November 20. Peace, love, and good health for the holidays.” Trending Stories Appeals Court Says Lawsuit Over ‘The Pitt’ Can Proceed, Cites ‘Numerous’ Similarities to ‘ER’ Nicki Minaj Is Going Down With Trump’s Ship After Nearly Every Candidate She Backed Lost As Nicki Minaj Rallied for Trump's GOP, Her Lawyers Told Court She Still Owes Them $295,000 Adam Lambert on His Future With Queen, New Music, and Why the Show Must Go On

Several commenters on his post from Wednesday took him to task for using generative AI as well. Rodgers defended its use, telling one commenter: “We created everything. Mood boards, story boards and did it the same way we’d do a film. The AI did what we told it to do and we painstakingly created every second you’ll see on the screen… If this were a traditional film it would’ve cost about $100,000.”

Rodgers told another commenter that “it’s somewhat hypocritical to say you’re anti AI and you’re using it all the time because it makes your life convenient,” he wrote. “I used to work with Microsoft and Apple. I’m very aware of the things they do when they have new tech that consumers like yourself adopt its usage. Love to you. I respect all opinions.”Rodgers has previously shown support for AI. In 2024, he told Euronews, “So, I think using AI-generated vocals to copy another person’s sound is horrible, but using AI to artistically express yourself and make better music, I think, is fantastic. The way I want to look at AI is from the positive side. I want to look at it as just another color on your palette,” he said, likening it to other tools. He cited how when drum machines and synthesizers first came out, people were saying it “wasn’t real music,” and how auto-tune helped shape the pop landscape. “I think that creativity comes from the artist. Now, if you have a tool to help you become more creative, there’s nothing wrong with that.” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century
